Benito Skinner’s ‘Overcompensating’ Returns for Season 2: More Laughs, More Sex, More Gay

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

It’s been a year and a half since the first season of Benito Skinner’s Prime Video comedy series, Overcompensating, debuted, and a lot has changed for him in that time. For one, Skinner—also known as Benny Drama, his online alter ego—is blond now. It’s a change he says may be permanent or perhaps semipermanent. “It’s how I see myself—as a blond,” he tells me over Zoom. “So we’re going to keep going with it for a sec.”

But Skinner has transformed in much less superficial ways since the launch of his hit Amazon series, which is based on his experiences coming out as gay as a Georgetown undergrad. Equal parts raunchy and heartwarming, the first season follows Benny (Skinner), a closeted former high school football player who attempts to navigate the chaos of college, where he forms an unlikely bond with a fellow student named Carmen (Wally Baram).

Skinner shares that making the second installment of the series was “way more fluid, way less stressed.” As it should have been. The first season was a popular hit for the streamer (it has a 93% fresh rating on Rotten Tomatoes), hard-launching Skinner as an exciting new voice plucked from the internet and ready for prime time.

Benny and Carmen begin the season as enemies.

The second season, which Vanity Fair can exclusively reveal will debut on February 17, 2027, expands the world he created with new storylines for the supporting characters—and bigger stakes for Benny as he comes into his own with his sexuality. Benny navigates not only coming out to his sister, Grace (Mary Beth Barone), and his parents (Connie Britton and Kyle MacLachlan), but also his own sexual exploration—and all the awkwardness that comes with it.

So much of what made the first season a hit—the awkward comedy, the silly themed parties, the talking Megan Fox poster—returns, but this time many of the characters grapple with deeper issues. Yet Skinner felt lighter making the show this time around. “I think in season one, Benny is that stressed, so that kind of worked,” he says. “But in this one, we’re having more fun. He has some joy, so we can loosen up a little bit.”

Connie Britton, center, reprises her role as Benny's mom.

After Overcompensating premiered, Skinner was approached, he says, by “a few gays” who told him, “I thought it’d be gayer, the show.” He promises that the second season delivers just that: “We’re creeping out of the closet,” says Skinner.

In the first season, Benny is trying to find his place on campus while constantly living in fear that the people around him will discover he’s gay. He falls for a dashing British classmate named Miles (Rish Shah), only to find out he is straight and has kissed Carmen. The season ends with Carmen revealing that she has also slept with Grace’s boyfriend, Peter (Adam DiMarco), and in the final moments, Carmen accidentally lets slip in front of Grace that Benny is gay.

Skinner and Lukas Gage reunite in season two.

In the upcoming season, Benny heads back home with his sister for the start of Christmas break, struggling because he hasn’t yet come out to his family. “If season one was about friends, I think this season is about family, and trying to reintroduce yourself to your parents after starting to become yourself,” says Skinner. The way his father finds out about it makes for an especially tense moment, and Benny goes to great lengths (like joining the crew team) to mend that bond with his dad. “That relationship between gay men and father, I think, is always fraught and confusing,” he says. “I just wanted to make it as honest as possible.”

At the start of the new season, Benny and Carmen are at odds; Grace and her boyfriend, Peter, have broken up; and many of the characters are exploring those feelings of loneliness that can crop up in college. A handful of exciting new faces also join this season. Australian actor Aisha Dee (The Bold Type) plays a new student who becomes an instant It girl on campus. AnnaSophia Robb (The Carrie Diaries) joins as an intense coxswain (the one who does the yelling) on the crew team. And Tony-nominated stage actor Tom Francis (Sunset Boulevard) plays the captain of the team, serving as Benny’s newest love interest.

Peter (Adam DiMarco) is looking for his path forward.

For Skinner, the second season is about capturing the ups and downs of coming out. It’s not just one reveal and then you’re out of the closet and everything is better. “I get to be even more specific about coming out and also tell a story that I think I haven’t seen yet done in this way, which is the complication of coming out—how you can take 10 steps forward, a hundred steps back,” he says.

Benny joins the crew team, where he meets a new love interest (Tom Francis).

There’s also a lot more sex. “There are a couple scenes that are definitely going to be off-limits for the family,” Skinner says. But of course, in Overcompensating, the sex scenes aren’t always smooth sailing. “I think our show is about trying to be sexy and then it going wrong, or feeling like you start to come into yourself but you get made a fool,” he says.

Skinner adds the title of executive music producer to his résumé this season. “It feels like a return to my radio-DJ roots, and that feels right,” he says. There’s more Britney Spears and Charli xcx (who appeared in a cameo last season), and he wanted the show’s music to remain an important part of the world. He even wrote a letter to Coldplay when he wanted to use the band’s song “Fix You” in this new season. “I love doing the letters to these guys because I’m like, So is Chris Martin reading this? Is this sent by owl? What’s the vibe?” he says with a smile.

Overcompensating’s return will come almost two years after its debut, and Skinner, who says he always plans to keep this story set in the characters’ college years, wants to make the wait a little shorter next time. “I apologize to everyone that it took a while,” he says. “TV takes a long time, so I’m trying to go faster, but daddy has to write it.”

“I don’t want any of these characters to feel like we’re pandering to maybe what we think the internet will want,” says Skinner of crafting the second installment after a hit first season.

“What I wanted to do is surprise people with where we took it,” says Skinner of the new season, “and have them be like, ‘Oh, this isn’t what I asked for, but it is something kind of delicious and new.’”

What would happen if a planet crashed into the Sun?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

What would happen if a planet crashed into the Sun?

This is a wonderful example of a thought experiment, the ultimate “what if?” question.

We should begin by pointing out that no fully formed planet will actually ever be able to crash into the Sun, as the Sun’s ferocious heat would begin to liquefy and vaporize the incoming world’s constituent matter as it approached, and tidal forces would tear it apart once it came too close. But let’s set aside that pesky astrophysical reality and imagine what would happen if a planet smashed into the Sun anyway.

According to Morgan MacLeod from the Harvard-Smithsonian Center for Astrophysics, the answer depends, logically enough, on the planet in question.

Cheerfully glossing over the dicey physics, we can say that if a terrestrial planet were to crash into the Sun, it is likely that we would not notice any dramatic effects — unless, of course, that planet were Earth. The consequent energy release could produce an event comparable to a fairly energetic CME, which could send a prodigious outflow of charged aurora-inducing particles speeding in our direction. In other words, it might produce a stunning sky show for us to enjoy.

On the other hand, if Jupiter crashed into the Sun, the ramifications would be catastrophic to us on Earth. Even if we step outside our imaginary scenario and assume that high temperatures and powerful tidal forces would tear Jupiter’s constituent material to shreds that would initially follow a lethargic orbit around the Sun before plunging precipitously into the Sun’s outer layers, the resultant energy release of the impact would cause the Sun to swell significantly. Although the extent and duration of this expansion are uncertain and minor in relative terms — less than 1 astronomical unit over a timeframe of around a century — the resultant increased solar luminosity would most likely extinguish all life on Earth, while a physically larger Sun would swallow Mercury and Venus. (One astronomical unit, or AU, is the mean separation between Earth and the Sun, or 93 million miles [150 million kilometers].)

So, toss Mercury into the Sun and we’ll see a bit of a blip. Toss Jupiter or Saturn into the Sun and we’ll see the end of life on Earth.

However, be reassured: At the time of this writing, Jupiter is 5.29 AU, or 492 million miles (791 million km) away from the Sun and locked into a stable orbit for billions of years to come. If it did somehow escape its orbit, its crashing into the Sun would be the least of our worries here on Earth, as its gravity would create a solar-system-wide apocalypse long before it reached our parent star.

Edward Herrick-GleasonAstronomy Educator, St. John’s, Newfoundland and Labrador

Nov. 9, 1939: The birth of Carl Sagan

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Nobody did more to popularize astronomy than American astronomer Carl Sagan, who was born Nov. 9, 1939, in New York. The landmark 1980 television series Cosmos: A Personal Voyage, which he cowrote and narrated, has been viewed by more than half a billion people. He also wrote popular science books, including The Dragons of Eden, Pale Blue Dot, and the novel Contact, which was turned into a motion picture.

But Sagan was no slouch scientifically. He predicted that Venus’ temperature should be above the melting point of lead due to a runaway greenhouse effect. He also imagined that Jupiter’s moon Europa might have an underground ocean.

As a member of the imaging team for NASA’s Voyager probes, Sagan hatched the idea for Voyager 1 to take the image that would become known as the Pale Blue Dot. In it, Earth is seen from a distance of 3.7 billion miles (6 billion kilometers). He later wrote, “Look again at that dot. That’s here. That’s home. That’s us. On it everyone you love, everyone you know, everyone you ever heard of, every human being who ever was, lived out their lives … on a mote of dust suspended in a sunbeam.”

Michael E. Bakich is a contributing editor for Astronomy magazine. With degrees in astronomy and planetarium education, he has led many eclipse tours, published several books on observing, and logged thousands of hours at the eyepiece.

Block Discloses Proposed $764M DOJ Payment in Anti-Money-Laundering Resolution

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Block, the payments company behind Cash App and Square, says it reached an agreement in principle with the US Department of Justice over an anti-money-laundering programme allegation. Its November 3 filing outlines approximately $964 million in gross monetary terms and a potential $764 million net payment after credits, but the resolution still requires a final agreement and court approval.

Crypto investors often think of compliance risk in terms of exchanges. A new filing from Block is a reminder that the same financial-crime obligations also shape mainstream payment apps that let customers use bitcoin alongside ordinary money transfers.

In its third-quarter Form 10-Q filed November 3, Block said it reached an agreement in principle during October with the US Department of Justice. The contemplated deferred prosecution agreement would address an allegation that the company failed to maintain an effective anti-money-laundering programme under the Bank Secrecy Act.

The filing describes a gross monetary component of roughly $964 million. Credits related to earlier regulatory resolutions would reduce the expected net payment to approximately $764 million, according to the company. Block says it accrued that latter amount as of September 30, 2026.

Under the contemplated arrangement, prosecutors would defer the relevant charge for three years. If the company fully complied with the obligations in a final agreement over that term, the DOJ would seek dismissal of the deferred charge.

None of this means the matter has already been settled in court. Block explicitly says the resolution is subject to a satisfactory final agreement and judicial approval. It also cautions that the company cannot guarantee the parties will reach final terms, and that losses could exceed what it has accrued.

That distinction matters in headlines. An agreement in principle is meaningful because it describes a possible route out of a substantial legal dispute. It is not the same as a completed final settlement or a judicial finding that every allegation has been resolved.

Block is best known for the Cash App and Square ecosystems. Cash App users can interact with bitcoin, but the company also provides payments, cards, lending and other financial tools. A breakdown in monitoring or due diligence controls can affect far more than a narrow crypto trading product.

The DOJ inquiry is connected to questions raised about the company’s compliance and risk practices following allegations aired in 2023. Block’s filing notes that a separate Securities and Exchange Commission investigation ended in March 2026 without a recommendation for enforcement action. The end of that separate inquiry should not be confused with the Justice Department’s proposed criminal resolution.

More broadly, investors have been considering the effect of tighter controls across crypto services. NewsBTC has covered stablecoin compliance restrictions affecting Coinbase customers, which addresses a different segment of the market but highlights why operational trust extends beyond fast transactions.

For a payments business, suspicious-activity monitoring, customer identification and escalation procedures must operate consistently at large scale. Even when individual transactions are small, the number of users and transfers can create a major supervisory challenge.

There is also an important distinction between financial penalties and technology investment. Paying a fine or reaching a deferred prosecution agreement does not by itself prove that controls have been fixed. Regulators and customers will look for the systems, staffing and governance needed to prevent a repeat.

The next step is whether Block and the DOJ complete a final agreement on the announced terms, including any independent monitoring or compliance obligations. Until then, the $764 million figure is the company’s expected net payment under a proposed resolution, not evidence that the case is fully closed.

The Rings Of Power Season 3 Will Have Some Unexpected Main Characters [Exclusive]

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Don't let the title fool you: "The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power" may suggest that this Prime Video series is all about the origins of Sauron (played by Charlie Vickers), but the truth is much more complicated than that. In the very first scene of Season 1, we see the events of the saga told through the eyes of Galadriel (Morfyyd Clark), positioning her as our de facto main protagonist. In the time since, an ensemble of characters have risen up to take the reins of this story. There's Robert Aramayo's Elrond, the Dwarf king Durin (Owain Arthur), our lovable clan of Harfoots, and so many more spread out over the vast expanse of Middle-earth. But, if you ask the show's creative team, Season 3 takes this versatile approach to even greater heights.

In fact, I did ask them exactly that. At the recent press junket for "The Rings of Power," I spoke to co-showrunner Patrick McKay about whether he sees these three seasons as a Sauron-centric narrative or something that extends far beyond our favorite Dark Lord. According to McKay, it's a tricky balance:

"Sauron is going to be the center of gravity in many ways, but it's an ensemble show, and we love the idea that from episode to episode, sequence to sequence, someone might surprise you [...] We love that we can do that."

McKay actually singled out one particular character by name who takes the lead at one point in "The Rings of Power" Season 3, but we'll save that (very minor) reveal for below. Needless to say, it's a perfect example of the series' writers staying light on their feet. With such a talented cast, the show might as well use them to its advantage.

Without spoiling any specifics, one of the more memorable instances of this comes in the early episodes of "The Rings of Power" Season 3. While Sauron's ongoing conquest of Middle-earth puts the pressure on the forces of good, the sequestered island nation of Númenor is dealing with its own troubles closer to home. Caught in the middle of this turmoil, the family made up of Isildur (Maxim Baldry), his sister Eärien (Ema Horvath), and their father Elendil (Lloyd Owen) are hard-pressed to keep up a united front. This subplot may not be the most obvious favorite for many viewers of the show, but Season 3 goes out of its way to shine a brighter spotlight on one secondary character in particular.

According to Patrick McKay, fans ought to be on the lookout for what Episode 2 has in store. "The second episode this season, arguably the protagonist is Ema Horvath's character Eärien over in Númenor — who you abstractly would never think that the sister of the [character] over in that particular story would take that kind of prominence," he explained. It's a calculated gamble, but one that speaks to his and co-showrunner J.D. Payne's new mentality. McKay added:

"We're just looking [for] ways to shake it up and surprise you, and we know what Ema can do. And we were very excited to give her that chance."

The storylines revolving around Galadriel, Sauron, and Jamie Campbell Bower's newly-introduced Celeborn will likely make most of the headlines this year, but don't sleep on the supporting characters populating this world. You never know which one might surprise you. "The Rings of Power" Season 3 premieres November 11, 2026, on Prime Video.

‘Half Nelson’ at 20: How a $700,000 Indie Addiction Drama Earned Ryan Gosling His First Oscar Nod

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

“He said it felt like we were the kind of people who talked about the script like we were plotting to build a bomb,” laughed Ryan Fleck, remembering how he and his longtime filmmaker partner, Anna Boden, convinced Ryan Gosling to star in their feature debut, “Half Nelson.” “It was probably a little bit true though,” adds Boden. “We hadn’t done much except try to make this movie for a long time, and maybe that came off the page. Maybe he was like, ‘Let’s just throw them a bone!’”

It was the right choice. “Half Nelson” arrived in 2006, an era when introspective indies still had time to flourish theatrically and find their crowd — and that’s exactly what happened. Filmed for just $700,000, Fleck and Boden’s first film turned a limited theatrical run into a $4.9 million global box office haul, while earning their leading man his first Best Actor Oscar nod. Looking back, it’s undoubtedly a success story — but far from a predictable one. Related Stories Cinematographers’ Favorite Festival Is All About AI This Year — but Artists Still Hate the Tech ‘You Can See Everything’ Sweeps Critics Choice Documentary Awards

“The thing about not yet being a professional filmmaker is you’ve got time until someone greenlights your movie, so there was lots of time,” said Fleck, recalling the movie’s long road to production. The pair met on a summer filmmaking course at NYU in the early aughts and quickly realized they wanted to work together. “We were always big movie fans, and the ones that moved me the most were films like ‘Midnight Cowboy’ and ‘Harold and Maude.’ They all had flawed people at their core. We started there and began digging into things we knew a little about.”

Together, they spliced their own flawed character with themes of isolation and addiction, and before long they had someone they felt they could form a feature around. Their idea centered on Dan Dunne (Gosling), a twenty-something middle-school teacher and struggling creative balancing full-time work with a freebasing addiction, and Drey (Shareeka Epps), the young student who stumbles on his secret. That was their starting point, but over time, it evolved.

“When we first started writing in 2002 or 2003, it felt like a lot of young people had all this energy but didn’t know how to affect change in the world around them,” said Boden, explaining how external conflicts like America’s involvement with the Afghanistan war impacted the story they wanted to tell. “We were finding this character that was going through that too, in his own way.”

Fleck unpacked this disconnect further: “[Dan] understands how history, politics and change work outside of himself but he can’t quite get it under control within himself.”

Despite having a finished screenplay, securing a green light proved elusive. However, instead of feeling dejected, the pair got to work. “We had this script but literally didn’t have anybody to give it to, so we made a short because that was something we did know how to do. We focused on the young student character, changing her from male to female and casting somebody from a local middle school,” said Boden, of Epps’ entry into the project. In fact, all the actors in their short “Gowanus, Brooklyn” were nonprofessionals. “It was a great way of working through the ideas, emotions and feelings we were trying to get across. Going back to the script after that deepened it a lot.”

Being proactive paid off. After a successful Sundance screening, “Gowanus, Brooklyn” bagged 2004’s Grand Jury Prize, paving the way for the feature they’d initially dreamed of — or so they thought. “I remember coming off stage and whispering to Anna, ‘I think this means we’re going to get to make our feature,’” said Fleck, laughing at his naivety. “It didn’t make it easy, but it certainly made it a more clear path.”

The win secured the duo an agent and key industry connections, through which their feature script reached their would-be leading man. Looking back, it’s easy to see someone as multi-faceted as Gosling deliver on the many faces this role required. Dan’s someone who’s simultaneously dejected, drug-addled and lost — but also inspirational, trusted and boasting a strong moral compass. Easy for someone with Gosling’s skillset, right? Fleck and Boden weren’t convinced right away.

“It had been written for someone a little bit older than him,” revealed Boden, describing the struggle of seeing the world-weary Dan in 24-year-old Gosling. “I remember when he met us, he’d grown his chin hairs out and looked a little older.” Fleck has similar memories: “It wasn’t immediate. We thought of somebody who had more life experience. He didn’t have the body of work he has now, but he just had this old-soul quality to him. He didn’t seem 24.”

Gosling threw himself into the role. “We introduced him to a public school teacher who was about his age so he could shadow him,” explained Boden. “Ryan picked up all these subtle little things from him, like wearing rubber bands around his wrists. The guy ended up looking so much like him that we cast him as Dan’s brother.” His ability to keep things loose lent itself to the pair’s fly-on-the-wall style, too. “Some of those classroom scenes have a very spontaneous feel,” she added, confirming that the students often didn’t know what Gosling was going to ask. “Some of their lines were scripted, some weren’t. They were reacting in real-time.”

Then there was the heavy stuff, like Drey unceremoniously discovering Dan’s freebasing habit in the school bathroom. As an inner-city teen, she’s caught in her own internal battle between a life of honest education and the quick wins of neighborhood crime, tempted by toxic family friend Frank (Anthony Mackie). It’s a challenging role for any actor, let alone someone making their feature debut.

“The other big thing [Ryan] brought was stripping away the dialogue. The scene where Drey is taking care of him after his overdose felt so much more visceral without any lines. You could hear a pin drop,” said Boden.

“I think Shareeka was genuinely frightened,” recalled Fleck. “There’s such an intensity… I think she was genuinely concerned for him — and I was concerned for her watching it. I was just blown away, like ‘Wow. These people are really good.’”

Meanwhile, low-level dealer Frank offers Drey his own guidance in a morally blurry role that’s part friend, part corruptor. “[Anthony] immediately got what we were going for,” said Boden. “Frank isn’t a cartel guy or a big kingpin. He’s your working-class drug dealer making just enough to live on. It’s something that should feel relatable, even though he’s doing something illegal. Anthony and Shareeka immediately clicked. You can feel it on screen.”

To score their first film, the duo drew inspiration from one of their favorites, Hal Ashby’s “Harold and Maude,” a film that dedicates its entire soundtrack to one artist: Cat Stevens. For “Half Nelson,” Fleck and Boden chose Canadian indie-rock collective Broken Social Scene, blending their stark imagery with a dreamy sound that delivered a deftly emotional punch.

“A friend of Anna’s introduced us to their music, and we became obsessed,” said Fleck, explaining the choice. “When you put their music against some of the images we were playing with, it gave us goosebumps.”

There was just one issue: They needed the band’s permission. Luckily, they had a connection to Broken Social Scene lead singer Kevin Drew — but the Canadian musician was a bit suspicious. “He made us watch the film with him in his living room,” laughed Fleck. “Thankfully, he really liked it and said, ‘OK, you can have all of our music.’”

Turns out, it was kismet. “When we played the song ‘Lover’s Spit’ for Ryan, he was like, ‘Oh my God, who’s this band?’” said Boden. “He’d been listening to a burned Broken Social Scene CD in his car for the past two months and didn’t know what it was because it wasn’t labeled. It was meant to be!”

Twenty years on, “Half Nelson” feels like a product of a bygone time, one that benefitted from having the space it needed to find its audience. As the movie prepares for a 2027 dual disc re-release and a return to theatres, its remarkable journey hasn’t gone unappreciated by its creators.

“It wasn’t a huge blockbuster. It played really well in independent cinemas and the people who wanted to see it saw it. I’m not sure it works like that anymore,” said Boden. “So I’m really grateful for the experience we had with ‘Half Nelson.’”

Fleck echoed the sentiment: “It was our first and the one that allowed us to continue doing this. I love everything about the process of making that movie and think back on everything really fondly.”

Samsung’s Newest Flagship Soundbar System Is $500 Off Ahead of Black Friday

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Samsung’s flagship soundbars have reached the point where the yearly upgrades are more about refinement than reinvention, and that is pretty much the story with the Samsung HW-Q990H—and with Black Friday approaching, this 2026 model is already seeing a $500 discount, bringing its price down to $1,497.99 from $1,997.99. That's its lowest price to date, according to price trackers.

Samsung kept the same 11.1.4-channel setup from the older HW-Q990F, including the soundbar, wireless subwoofer, and two rear speakers, but leaned harder into software this time around—features like the SpaceFit Sound Pro automatically adjust the sound depending on your room layout, and unlike some auto-calibration features that barely change anything, this one reportedly shifts the audio balance in noticeable ways. Sometimes it improves dialogue clarity and bass response. Other times, you may prefer tweaking things yourself with the built-in seven-band EQ.

The system also handles basically every major audio format, supports wireless Dolby Atmos with compatible Samsung TVs, and includes 4K 120Hz HDMI passthrough for people gaming on a PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series X. That said, while the bass is strong for a compact subwoofer, it compresses when you push the volume near maximum, so it's not perfect for huge spaces or people who want nightclub-level output. More importantly, if you already own the HW-Q990F or even the older HW-Q990D, upgrading probably will not feel dramatic enough to justify the cost. But for someone starting from TV speakers or an entry-level soundbar, this system can completely change how movies, games, and even sports feel at home.

Black Friday sales officially begin Friday, Nov. 27, 2026, and run throughout “Cyber Week,” the five-day period that runs from Thanksgiving through Cyber Monday,  Nov. 30, 2026. But Black Friday and Cyber Monday dates have expanded as retailers compete for customers. You can get the same Black Friday sales early, and we expect sales to wind down by Dec. 2, 2026.

Nowadays, both large retailers and small businesses compete for Black Friday shoppers, so you can expect practically every store to run sales through Monday, Nov. 30, 2026. The “best” sales depend on your needs, but in general, the biggest discounts tend to come from larger retailers who can afford lower prices: think places like Amazon, Walmart, Target, Best Buy, and Home Depot. You can find all the best sales from major retailers on our Black Friday coverage.

Yes, Amazon has Black Friday sales, but prices aren’t always what they seem. Use a price tracker to make sure you’re getting the best deal, or refer to guides like our live blog that use price trackers for you. And if you have an Amazon Prime membership, make the most of it.

Cisse like the greats, Amorim surpasses Allegri: All the key stats from Genoa 1-2 AC Milan

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

AC Milan won their seventh Serie A game of the season on Sunday afternoon, beating a Genoa side that entered the game in great form.

The home crowd at the Marassi were fired up going into this one given Genoa had won four of their last five. However, goals from Alphadjo Cisse and Christian Pulisic inside the opening 20 minutes was exactly what Milan needed to take the ascendancy.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Ellertsson did score inside the final few minutes as Genoa mounted a late surge in the hopes of snatching a point, but the Rossoneri held on to win 2-1 and move back into the top four places in the table.

Looking at the general stats makes for interesting reading. Milan dominated the ball with 62% possession, but Genoa had nearly double the Expected Goals (1.14 to 0.61), even though big chances ended up being even at two apiece.

The Rossoblu also had nearly triple the shots (17 to 6) and shots on target (5 to 2), while the Rossoneri did not win a single corner. It suggests that Ruben Amorim’s side simply took their moments and sat back, though maybe Daniele De Rossi can feel a bit hard done by.

📊 The stats from the win against Genoa. pic.twitter.com/wr1E6cohcZ

— MilanData📊 (@acmilandata) November 8, 2026

Alphadjo Cisse is one of only three players born in 2006 or later to have scored 8+ goals across all competitions in Europe’s top five leagues. The others are Lamine Yamal (13) and Paris Brunner (8).

In terms of minutes per goal in Serie A, Cisse leads the way with one every 81 minutes. That puts him ahead of the likes of Donyell Malen (one every 82), Lautaro Martinez (one every 88) and Gustavo Varela (one every 91).

After 13 appearances for Milan, Cisse is level with Filippo Inzaghi, Andriy Shevchenko and Alberto Gilardino on 8 goals. Pierino Prati (10) and Mario Balotelli (12) are the only players to record more.

Milan are unbeaten in their last 11 Serie A matches against Genoa (W7, D4). Since 2020-21, the Rossoneri only have a longer run of unbeaten games against Hellas Verona (12).

The Diavolo have won each of their last five Serie A away games against Genoa. Only against one team do the Rossoneri currently have a longer running away win streak in the competition: Hellas Verona (seven).

Milan have won three and drawn three of their first six away games this season. The Rossoneri have pnly avoided defeat in their first six away games of the season on three occasions between 2020-21 and 2025-26, the same number as Inter and Napoli, and could now become the only team to do so four times in this span, including the current season.

With this victory, AC Milan climbed to 24 points in the standings, two more than the number earned by Massimiliano Allegri’s Milan last season after 11 games. This is also better than Stefano Pioli’s last season, also by two points.

Boardroom Promotes Sarah Flynn to President (Exclusive)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Flynn helped launch the media company and has been chief marketing officer for the past several years.

Sarah Fynn has been named president of Boardroom, the sports media and entertainment company co-founded by NBA star Kevin Durant and longtime manager Rich Kleiman.

Flynn rose up to the position after helping launch Boardroom with Durant and Kleiman and serving as chief marketing officer for the company for the past five-and-a-half years. In the new role, Flynn will oversee business strategy, marketing, partnerships, experiential and content operations for Boardroom, as well as 35V, Durant’s family office, which makes investments in startups and supports his personal brand and family foundation.

As CMO, Flynn struck strategic brand partnerships with Fanatics, Yahoo! Sports and CNBC, among others, and built Boardroom’s events slate including the inaugural Baha Mar Leadership Summit in September 2026 and the annual CNBC x Boardroom Game Plan Summit. She has also overseen the production of experiences surrounding NBA All-Star, WNBA All-Star, Super Bowl and the US Open. Flynn also launched Boardroom’s Members Club in June 2025 and its quarterly print magazine with Condé Nast in April 2026.

Earlier in her career, she was instrumental in Boardroom’s early marketing and content strategy which included producing credits on the Academy Award-winning Two Distant Strangers (Netflix), the critically acclaimed scripted series Swagger (Apple TV+) and Emmy-nominated documentary NYC Point Gods (Showtime). She is an executive producer on the forthcoming Kevin Durant documentary series with Netflix.

Prior to Boardroom and 35V, Flynn served as senior director of sports marketing at Roc Nation, where she helped launch Roc Nation Sports.

“When I joined 35V in 2017, Rich, Kevin and I knew we wanted to build in the media space but that we wanted to take the time to make sure we did it right. That’s paid off in so many ways as we look at what Boardroom has become and the incredible network that we have built. I’m extremely proud of the team we’ve built that continues to evolve the brand forward and expand our community,” Flynn said.

“Sarah, Kevin and I built Boardroom together from the beginning,” Kleiman said. “Her impact has always extended well beyond marketing across every area of our business, and elevating her to President is a natural next step as we continue to grow Boardroom. I can’t think of anyone better suited to help lead us into this next chapter.”

Automattic debuts a platform to deploy vibe coding apps amid a changing internet

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

WordPress has been a crucial technology and content management system for websites like TechCrunch. Now, WordPress.com owner Automattic is responding to the changing state of the web by debuting a new service called Spacefast, which will allow users to deploy vibe-coded websites and apps.

Spacefast is designed to provide infrastructure for the vibe-coded apps, including databases, authentication, file uploads, emails, and analytics, to make them functional websites, apps, or services. The product competes with the likes of Lovable and Emergent, which offer both interface and rails for vibe-coded apps, but in a more open way.

Users can connect Spacefast with other agents and chatbots like ChatGPT, Claude, Hermes, OpenClaw, Grokbot, Muse, Pi, Cursor, Opencode, and Amp. Alternatively, they can connect a Git repository to Spacefast to get started.

The product is headed by Batuhan İçöz, who worked on working on it during one of the company’s hackathon sprints earlier in the year. At that time, he worked on the company’s cross-platform messaging app Beeper and said that he often got Vercel links from his non-technical friends while exploring vibe coding.

“I thought there had to be a better way for everyone to set up and deploy their projects. Automattic already has good infrastructure, but we have been traditionally associated with just WordPress. I thought, why do I have to use Cloudflare when we have a CDN (Content Delivery Network) just as good? So I started working on what was called Stattic and now Spacefast,” he told TechCrunch over a call.

İçöz eventually disseminated the solution to his friends and added more features to make the product more dynamic. Automattic soft-launched the product in September to get feedback before today’s launch. The company has seen varied use cases of their product, with people and organizations using it for internal mockups, slideshows, and offer-related websites. However, that’s not the only competition Spacefast has. Productivity and design software like Figma, Canva, and Notion have launched products that sit at the intersection of vibe coding, mockups, and presentations.

İçöz thinks that, compared to something like Lovable, Spacefast has the advantage of giving users a choice of their agent and using the solution their way. The counter-argument might be that a certain kind of user wouldn’t want to choose and just have everything figured out for them.

Spacefast said that it also has the advantage of being within Automattic to launch solutions like web analytics, WordPress integration, and e-commerce features (through WooCommerce) easily to compete better with the whole lot.

“For enterprises, the problem right now is IT sprawl, as their marketing teams are churning out landing pages on services like Lovable, and they want to consolidate and work with a company that understands the enterprise needs. With our existing products, we have experience serving enterprise customers,” İçöz noted.

A challenge for newly minted products in the AI world is to retain people post-launch. Users might get excited to try out a new product, and then move on to the new shiny thing within days or weeks. İçöz is cognizant of the fact and said that he expects people to churn, as they might not be interested in making apps at all.

“I think what we need to do as a product is make sure that when you build something with SpaceFast, it is so easy, and the results are so much better than without Spacefast,” İçöz emphesized.

Many websites and publishers are struggling with dwindling traffic due to AI chatbots and summaries, right now, to the point that Google itself saw its ad revenue growth drop. İçöz acknowledged that people are making websites with ChatGPT or Claude now as compared to deploying something on WordPress, and he said some sites won’t need a content management system at all as the web expands.

Spacefast comes amid turbulent months at Automattic. In an OpenAI-Sam Altman-style saga, the company’s board forced CEO Matt Mullenweg out, but he was back in control within days. The rebel board was eventually replaced, and the interim CFO left within weeks of appointment. In October, TechCrunch reported that the company had began layoffs across several properties and more cuts were expected.

Meet Drops, the new music app bringing back the fun of sharing songs with friends

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

There’s a new music discovery app borrowing the endless vertical-scrolling format of TikTok. But instead of watching videos, users swipe through short clips of songs, hoping to stumble upon their next favorite artist.

Launched Monday by Harman, a Samsung subsidiary, Drops aims to make discovering new music feel social again.

Available on iOS and Android in the U.S., the app lets users scroll through 10-to-30-second snippets of songs, accompanied by lyrics, album artwork, artist photos, and captions. The idea is to give listeners just enough of a track to grab their attention, whether it’s a particularly good lyric, a catchy hook, or a beat that makes them want to hear more.

The company says Drops is meant to feel like a return to how people used to discover music in the first place. Before every platform’s algorithm recommendations, people often heard about new songs through friends, music blogs, or online communities. Drops is trying to recreate that experience.

“You send a friend a song and say, ‘You have to hear this.’ Then you end up texting, ‘Skip to 1:47, that’s the best part.’ The part that hits usually isn’t the first few seconds, and there wasn’t a good way to share just that moment,” Clare Hunter, general manager of Drops, told us.

Hunter explained that the idea for Drops began taking shape after Harman acquired music management software company Roon in 2023.

“I used to spend hours making mixtapes for my friends, picking the songs, the order, and decorating the cover. Drops is the version of that I always wanted,” Hunter added.

The app is easy to navigate, with tabs including “For You,” “New,” “Showcase,” and “Following,” along with relevant and trending hashtags.

Users can like, comment, repost, share, and save tracks to collections called Droplists. They can also send songs directly to other users, follow other profiles, and exchange direct messages.

To listen to the entire track, Drops offers integrations with major streaming services, including Apple Music, Qobuz, and TIDAL. Users can jump directly into the full version of the song through their connected streaming platform.

Notably, the company says it doesn’t use generative AI for audio or visuals. Songs appear as they were originally released, using official album artwork and licensed artist photography. At a time when AI-generated music and slop are becoming harder to avoid, this will likely appeal to many users.

Another part worth noting is that Drops pays rights holders and reports plays back to its partners. Those partners include Universal Music Group, Beggars Group, Empire, Symphonic Distribution, and UnitedMasters.

Plays also count toward streams on those services, meaning Drops isn’t trying to replace existing music subscriptions.

Drops is currently invite-only, with U.S. users able to join the waitlist.

Musk says Cybertruck isn’t dying from weak sales — the numbers disagree

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Elon Musk now claims Cybertruck orders “far exceed” Tesla’s production rate, and that the truck isn’t being killed because it’s unprofitable. It’s his second explanation in three days for ending the pickup.

The problem is that Tesla’s own numbers say the opposite. The Cybertruck line has been running at roughly a quarter of its installed capacity, and Tesla spent all of 2026 discounting, re-trimming, and repricing the truck to find buyers.

“The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate. We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people. This is not a case of Cybertruck being unprofitable, as has been misreported by legacy media, but rather that the opportunity cost of the complexity of Cybertruck is vastly outweighed by the value of incremental Cybercab production.”

The Cybertruck order rate far exceeds our production rate. We will try to make as many as possible before shutting down the line in about 8 months, but will have to return deposits for a lot of people.This is not a case of Cybertruck being unprofitable, as has been misreported… https://t.co/rGmNKV3ILs

He was quote-tweeting a Tesla fan telling people to “get it while you can.” So yes, this is also a sales pitch.

On Friday, Musk announced Tesla would wind down Cybertruck production at Giga Texas around mid-2027 “to make room for increased Cybercab production.” That was the first story. Now it’s about “complexity” and “opportunity cost.” Let’s go through it.

Tesla controls its production rate. That’s the flaw in this claim.

Tesla built the Cybertruck supply chain for 250,000 units a year, and its latest shareholder letter still lists more than 125,000 units of installed capacity. In Q3 2026, it delivered 8,295 “Other Models.” With Model S and Model X out of production, we estimated 7,000 to 8,000 of those were Cybertrucks. That’s a 30,000-a-year pace, down 48% year-over-year in that category.

If orders had been outrunning production at any point this year, Tesla had about 95,000 units of idle annual capacity sitting right there. It didn’t use it.

And companies with a backlog don’t behave like Tesla did. In February, it offered a dual-motor Cybertruck at $59,990 for “only the next 10 days.” It pulled a single-motor RWD version after just 173 sales. It leaned on Musk’s own companies too: SpaceX bought 1,279 Cybertrucks in Q4 2025, about 19% of US registrations that quarter once you add his other firms.

Cox Automotive estimates US Cybertruck sales fell from 14,416 in Q3 2024 to 3,519 in Q1 2026 and 3,744 in Q2. It’s a demand problem everyone could see.

So if orders spiked this weekend, there’s an obvious reason: Musk told the world on Friday that the truck is going away. A last-call rush on a discontinued vehicle is not proof that it was selling.

Tesla has been sitting on Cybertruck deposits it was never going to fill for years.

Musk touted more than a million reservations, and crowdsourced tallies put the list near 2 million by 2023. Its best US quarter, by Cox Automotive’s estimate, was 14,416 trucks. The reservation book was a vanity number, not an order book.

Two weeks ago, Tesla cancelled the Cybertruck for Australia and New Zealand and refunded deposits people had paid in 2019. Returning deposits is something Tesla was already doing because it couldn’t sell the truck in those markets, not because it was swamped.

This one is a straw man. Our reporting, and most of the coverage Friday, was about sales, not margins. The truck sells at about 12% of the volume Tesla planned for.

Tesla doesn’t disclose Cybertruck margins, so Musk can say whatever he wants here and nobody can check it. But he’s the one who said in 2023 that Tesla had “dug our own grave with Cybertruck” because of how hard it was to build profitably.

And look at what Tesla did the same day it announced the end of production: it raised Cybertruck prices by up to 10% and paused orders for the cheapest trim. The cheapest orderable Cybertruck went from $69,990 in early August to $92,490. You do that to squeeze margin out of the last trucks, not because the line was printing money.

Follow Musk’s logic to the end. If the Cybertruck were profitable and orders really outran production, killing it would mean walking away from guaranteed profit for a robotaxi that had 463 units registered in Texas as of late October and no proven business yet.

The only way that trade makes sense is if the Cybertruck’s profit at 30,000 units a year is small. Which is another way of saying it doesn’t sell enough.

Giga Texas also has more than 10 million square feet of floor space, and the Cybercab runs on its own “unboxed” line. Tesla can’t make use of the Cybertruck line and floor space was never the constraint. To further adapt his logic for shutting down Cybertruck production, Musk shifted his argument from “space” to Cybercab’s line needing some “smart people”, who he implied work on the Cybertruck.

Musk wrote on Saturday that “there are a very limited number of smart, dedicated people”, but Michael So, who led Cybercab new product introduction and was Giga Texas employee #1, left in April for nuclear startup Aalo Atomics. Last month, Tesla lost its Cybercab general assembly lead to Rivian’s R2 program just weeks after the launch. If talent is the bottleneck, it’s the Cybercab team that’s bleeding it.

Musk doesn’t want the Cybertruck to go down in history as a flop, and we get it. It’s his truck. He approved the controversial design, hyped it for four years before it shipped, and boasted that 1 million reservations could translate to 500,000 units a year.

But you can’t run a line at a quarter of capacity, run “10 days only” discounts, pull trims after 173 sales, and sell trucks to your own rocket company, then turn around and tell people demand was never the issue. The data is public. Tesla’s own delivery reports are public.

Our read on what happened is simple. Demand collapsed in 2025 and never came back. Tesla tried every pricing trick it had. When nothing worked, it found a future-facing reason to end it, and now it’s spinning the inevitable end-of-life rush as proof the truck was a hit all along.

Musk loves a big TAM. SpaceX’s IPO filing put its total addressable market at $28.5 trillion, and last month he went further, writing that he sees “a path to SpaceX being worth orders of magnitude more than the current Earth economy.”

So apply the same lens to Tesla. Pickups are one of the biggest and most profitable vehicle markets on the planet. Ford sold 801,525 F-Series trucks in the US last year, GM sold 925,656 Silverados and Sierras combined, and Ram moved 374,059. That’s more than 2.1 million trucks from three automakers, easily north of $100 billion a year at today’s transaction prices.

Its funny that such a "free speech absolutist" won't disclose something as simple as auto only OpMargins...proving my point that Tesla post-Musk buyout simply existed to make products that were eligible for EV and green tax credits.

Robotaxis? Waymo, by far the leader, is doing about 500,000 paid rides a week, and analysts estimated its revenue run rate at roughly $355 million earlier this year. Tesla doesn’t even break out robotaxi revenue and we expect it to be a fraction of Waymo’s.

Maybe that flips one day. But that’s the bet here: Tesla is walking away from a giant market it can sell into today for one that only gets big if autonomy is solved in the next couple of years. Musk has made that exact bet before. He promised a million robotaxis on the road by 2020 and has said unsupervised self-driving was a year away almost every year since 2016. He’s lost every time.

And that rush is real, for what it’s worth. Some people want the last of a weird, genuinely impressive truck. That doesn’t make it a business.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. United Airlines Opens First New Concourse In 20 Years At Washington Dulles Airport

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

It's no secret that United Airlines is by far the dominant operator at Washington Dulles International Airport (IAD). According to data from Cirium, an aviation analytics company, the US 'big three' legacy carrier and Star Alliance founding member accounts for 7,830 of the 10,336 flights that are scheduled to depart from Dulles this month (76%). This includes those operated by its regional partners.

The airline's commitment to the airport was underlined on Monday as it previewed Concourse E, a brand-new part of Dulles that marks its first new concourse in close to 20 years. United Airlines unveiled the 435,000-square-foot (40,413-square-meter) concourse with the Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority, with this development marking part of a wider $20 billion investment in the carrier's DC hub.

Following Monday's preview, Concourse E will officially open to the public on Tuesday. In a corresponding statement that it released earlier today, United Airlines said that, among other things, this new area will offer "14 new gates, direct AeroTrain access, and new dining and retail options" to its passengers flying from and through the capital. As a result, it will be able to operate 300+ daily flights next year.

The upshot of this is the fact that United Airlines will subsequently be able to "grow its connectivity to other markets by nearly 30% during peak travel times, giving customers more flights and new destinations from Dulles." Concourse E will continue to grow, with United targeting 36 more gates and an international customs processing area in future phases. Scott Kirby, the CEO of United Airlines, said that:

"It's only the beginning as we work with the Airports Authority and the US Department of Transportation to transform Dulles into one of the greatest airports in the world."

Taking up almost 10% of the new real estate in Concourse E, arguably the most impressive aspect of this latest expansion is the new United Club lounge. Located across from gates E14 and E16 and open every day from 5:30 AM to 10:00 PM, this sprawling facility measures close to 40,000 square feet (3,617 square meters), making it the second-largest Club lounge in the United network.

Such is its size that United's overall lounge footprint at Dulles is set to grow by some 70% upon its public opening on Tuesday. This, the airline says, will "further elevate the experience for United customers traveling through the hub." Drawing upon the city's character, it features a collection of specially curated artwork from local artists, which will "bring a distinct sense of place to the travel experience."

In terms of what visitors can expect, United promises impressive connectivity, with "complimentary high-speed Wi-Fi and convenient power access at nearly every seat." Eight private phone rooms are also available for more discreet conversations. As always, food and drink will play an important role in bringing this lounge experience to life, with a bar and two buffet stations ensuring that flyers stay refreshed.

Dulles just tripled its modernization budget to more than $20 billion, with Concourse C and D set for replacement.

As previously noted, United accounts for 76% of Dulles' departures this month, with a grand total of some 7,830 flights scheduled. Of these, 1,058 are international, with Toronto (YYZ) being the most frequently served foreign destination (three times a day). United also flies twice a day from Dulles to Cancun (CUN), Frankfurt (FRA), London (LHR), Montreal (YUL), Ottawa (YOW), and Punta Cana (PUJ).

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

Domestically speaking, San Francisco (SFO) is United's top destination from Dulles, with up to nine flights a day. Elsewhere, Denver (DEN) peaks at eight daily rotations, compared to seven for Chicago O'Hare (ORD) and Los Angeles (LAX). While the scale of United's ops at Dulles is certainly impressive, the DC airport is actually only its sixth-busiest hub by flights. Chicago O'Hare sits first, with 18,920.

Man City transfer news: Etihad player ‘finished’ under Enzo Maresca with January plans revealed

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

By Oliver Thomas | 09 Nov 2026 13:56

Manchester City defender Rayan Ait-Nouri is reportedly ‘finished’ at the Etihad Stadium having fallen down the pecking order under manager Enzo Maresca.

Ait-Nouri was signed by Maresca’s predecessor Pep Guardiola in the summer of 2025, joining from Wolverhampton Wanderers in a £31m deal and penning a five-year contract.

The 25-year-old left-back chipped in with five assists in 28 appearances for City across all competitions last season, missing the majority of December and January following his involvement with Algeria at the Africa Cup of Nations.

Ait-Nouri celebrated winning both the FA Cup and EFL Cup in his debut campaign with the Sky Blues, though he did not feature in either final played at Wembley.

This term, Ait-Nouri has struggled to force his way into Maresca’s first-team plans and is currently viewed as a third-choice left-back behind Josko Gvardiol and Nico O'Reilly.

Ait-Nouri is yet to make a single Premier League appearance for Man City in 2026-27, while he has made just two substitute appearances in the Champions League – his only two starts have come in the EFL Cup.

Although Ait-Nouri is said to remain keen to fight for his place at Man City, Football Insider claims that the defender has failed to make an impression under Maresca.

The report adds that Ait-Nouri is unlikely to play ahead of either Gvardiol or O’Reilly any time soon, and could therefore push for a move away from City during the January transfer window in search of regular first-team football.

Maresca recently admitted that Ait-Nouri is struggling for game time, telling reporters: "He's a clear full-back. In that position, we have [Gvardiol], who is doing fantastic, we have [O'Reilly], who when he is fit is doing fantastic; and he's the same position, so it's just competition between them."

A surprise transfer to City’s rivals Manchester United was mooted for Ait-Nouri in the closing stages of the summer window, and the possibility of the Red Devils making a January swoop is said to have been explored.

While agreeing personal terms would seemingly not be an issue for Ait-Nouri, who could be receptive to a switch across Manchester, it is understood that the relationship between City and United is the ‘main issue’ that could scupper this potential move.

Serie A giants Juventus have also been consistently linked with a move for Ait-Nouri and a switch to Italy is thought to be more realistic at this stage, though the Old Lady may prefer to negotiate an initial loan deal.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Employee-owned solar and energy storage developer ECA Power, Luminace, ComEd, construction partner Miller Bros., and local community leaders celebrated the energization of the 7.4-MWDC Rockford Community Energy Initiative (RCEI), a community solar project in Cherry Valley, Illinois.

“The Illinois General Assembly has spent the past several years passing legislation to encourage and incentivize renewable energy projects like this one,” said State Sen. Steve Stadelman. “It’s always exciting to work on legislation and then see that work translate into real development on the ground.”

“Northern Illinois is becoming a national leader in community solar, and projects like this are helping bring the benefits of clean energy to more families and businesses,” said Gil Quiniones, president and CEO of ComEd. “We’re proud to work with ECA Power and Luminace to connect projects like this to the grid, and build a power system that can support continued clean energy growth across our region.”

RCEI was built in a state-designated R3 area, bringing new investment to a community targeted for revitalization. As part of its community benefit agreement with the Village of Cherry Valley, ECA Power contributed $150,000 to support the buildout, operation and maintenance of an EV charging station.

“At ECA Power, we believe the success of a project should be measured not only by the energy it produces, but by the lasting value it creates for the surrounding community,” said Todd Fryatt, founder and CEO of ECA Power. “The Rockford Community Energy Initiative reflects that commitment by expanding access to affordable, locally generated energy while bringing meaningful investment to the region. From supporting local revitalization to our partnership with the Village of Cherry Valley, we’re proud to deliver a project that will benefit this community for years to come.”

Home listings pile up in October, but sellers won't slash prices

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Homebuyers continue to retreat, but sellers are still digging in, refusing to cut prices or give up. As a result, September's frozen housing market is now October's frozen and increasingly bloated market.

Active inventory in October hit 183,500 homes, up 7.4% year-over-year, according to Realtor.com. That is the fastest annual growth in eight months. Active listings have been rising each month since February. This is despite the fact that new listings are actually falling, down 7.5% from October 2025.

The market has seen several years of extremely lean supply, driven by the homebuying frenzy during the first two years of the pandemic, when mortgage rates hit more than a dozen record lows. Now supply nationally is just 6.6% below typical pre-pandemic levels, the narrowest gap since the pandemic.

"The market is cooling on both sides, but in different ways. Buyers are stepping back as higher mortgage rates cut into their buying power, while sellers are listing less often without a broad increase in delistings or price cuts," said Danielle Hale, chief economist at Realtor.com. "We're seeing a pause in new supply, but owners who already have homes on the market appear to be largely unphased."

Just over 20% of sellers cut prices in October, about the same as both September and October of last year. There was also no meaningful increase in delistings by sellers.

Mortgage rates came down a little bit in October month-to-month, but they are still over a full percentage point higher than a year ago. That continued to weigh on sales. Newly pending sales, representing signed contracts, not closings, dropped 16.8% in October year-over-year, according to Zillow. That was the biggest annual drop since August 2023 and slowest October since 2018.

While rates fell slightly, home prices continued to gain. Zillow estimated the monthly mortgage payment on the typical U.S. home was $2,004 in October, assuming a 20% down payment and excluding taxes and insurance. That is an increase of 12.8% from last year and 4.8% higher than in September, the biggest one-month jump in four years.

"November is shaping up to have an even larger contraction, but the silver lining is that next month is likely going to be the worst of it," said Mischa Fisher, Zillow's chief economist, in a release. "I expect the pace of the slowdown to ease into the new year. There are deals to be had for buyers able to brave the affordability headwinds in the coming months, and the continued inventory buildup means there is a lot of upside for sales into next year if we get rate relief."

Analysts see no broader ‘contagion’ from $6.3B Binance hack

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Binance’s pledge to cover all user losses helped contain the fallout from the $6.3 billion hack, analysts said.

Binance’s pledge to cover user losses from its $6.3 billion hack helped contain the fallout across crypto markets, analysts told Cointelegraph.

“There doesn’t seem to be any contagion-type effect from the hack, given that Binance is able to cover the losses using its balance sheet,” Martin Lee, market insights lead at DWF Labs, told Cointelegraph. He said investors turned their focus to the implications of the US midterm elections.

Binance’s daily net outflows fell to $458 million in the 24 hours leading to Monday’s publication time, down from $6.1 billion on Nov. 2 after withdrawals resumed, according to DefiLlama.

Attackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance hot wallets in the Oct. 29 breach, making it the largest crypto hack to date. Bitcoin (BTC) fell as much as 5.7% over the first five days after the hack, a decline CryptoQuant called “modest” in its Wednesday report .

Bitcoin fell roughly 13.6% after the $1.4 billion Bybit hack in 2025 and about 9.9% after the Coincheck hack in 2018. At least 14 of the past 38 hacks CryptoQuant reviewed were followed by steeper Bitcoin declines.

Bitcoin’s drawdown after the Binance hack, compared to previous exchange hacks. Source: CryptoQuant

Major exchange hacks have previously triggered “short-lived drawdowns that markets later absorbed” as long-term holder demand returned, Lacie Zhang, research lead at Bitget Wallet, told Cointelegraph, adding that the Binance hack does not represent a “structural trend reversal” for crypto markets.

Related: Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

“Much of the hack’s impact now looks priced in,” with Bitcoin currently trading at an “attractive level,” Bitfinex analysts told Cointelegraph.

“Confirmation of a market bottom now depends on the Consumer Price Index (CPI) reading due on 10 November, which will shape the Federal Reserve’s next rate decision. Higher bond yields would put pressure on risk assets.”

Market participants priced in an 80% chance that the Federal Reserve will raise rates at its Dec. 9 meeting, up from 69% a month earlier, according to the CMEGroup’s FedWatch tool.

Bitcoin fell 2.5% over 24 hours to $74,580 as of 1:21 pm UTC on Monday, according to CoinMarketCap.

Student journalists are reporting more local news for real audiences. Their professors are exhausted.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

A new study finds that journalism school faculty — charged with putting more student work in front of non-student readers — often serve as a hidden infrastructure for local news. Burnout is a risk.

If there’s one big takeaway from the past two decades of local news struggle — the often perilous, occasionally brilliant transition from dominant newspapers to online media — perhaps it’s this: Passion alone isn’t enough.

A newspaper newsroom filled with committed editors and brilliant reporters still stares into the same headwinds as its more middling peers. The devotion of those leading a nonprofit outlet can mean little if revenue can’t keep the doors open. And even when the money is less important — when journalism is somewhat protected from the demands of the market — passion is still a finite resource, and it’s not always renewable.

That truism came to mind when I was reading a recent article by Sima Bhowmik of the University of Vermont and Andrea Lorenz of Kent State. It looks at one specific corner of the contemporary news ecosystem: journalism schools that use their students to fill voids in local coverage.

J-schools, after all, have faculties full of experience and students who need it. A few decades ago, an early-career journalist might have spent a few years covering city council meetings and school board budgets before moving up the chain to a bigger outlet. That path barely exists these days, and journalism programs know their students need the chance to do real work. What if these reporters-in-training could handle some of the local stories that the city’s daily no longer can?

RELATED ARTICLEA “win-win” partnership brings a surge of reporting firepower to hyperlocal news outlets around BostonSarah ScireNovember 13, 2025RELATED ARTICLELen Downie: The teaching hospital model works, but it will require drastic changeLeonard Downie Jr.September 12, 2012If you happen to live near a college with a journalism program, you’ve probably seen this idea — often called the “teaching hospital” model — in action. Here in the Boston suburbs, many of the stories I read about my town are written by Boston University students. According to UVM’s Center for Community News, where Bhowmik is a post-doc research fellow, there were 282 such efforts as of 2025, producing local news inside 205 colleges and universities. Those numbers were up from 227 programs in 172 colleges the year before — a boom fueled by a surge in philanthropic funding for these partnerships.

Bhowmik and Lorentz’s paper — published in Journalism & Mass Communication Educator — is the result of interviews with 118 faculty and staff at 101 of those colleges, and their major takeaway is one that will be familiar to many in local news: They’re exhausted.

Participants repeatedly used phrases such as “at capacity,” “don’t have the bandwidth,” “not enough hours of the day,” and “it’s just a lot” to describe the workload. Several described long workdays and exhaustion, with one participant saying the amount of editing “just about kills me every semester.” At many institutions, faculty workload was intensified by staffing shortages, retirements, hiring freezes, and budget cuts. This left faculty with little time to provide the one-on-one coaching necessary to elevate student stories to publishable quality… “What’s happening in the academic ecosystem is so phenomenal, thanks to these really passionate founders on the faculty,” Bhowmik told me. “But the extra work they’re putting it isn’t always getting acknowledged within the system. A professor might be editing 10 or 20 stories a week, by students without much experience, on top of a full teaching load. There’s a lot of overwork and some dissatisfaction, even though they know how important the work is.” (See above: Passion alone isn’t enough.) Here’s how one faculty member described their situation: Think about it: Like, how many reporters does the average assigning editor at a mid-sized newspaper supervise? Half a dozen? So try, like, 20 of them, and each of them is writing for a different outlet maybe, which has its own set of deadlines and editor. And they’re all at varying skill levels, and broadly, they’re all pretty new. Their copy requires a lot of work, their reporting often requires a lot of handholding. Another talked about the responsibility of getting student work up to the level a news outlet expects. You can’t just give a meh student story a C- and be done with it if a local editor is expecting publishable copy. It takes a lot of work to get student work up to the quality of professionally published work. And a lot of students start out with really good ideas, do some good reporting by the time they’re done in the semester, it needs work, and that tends to fall on the faculty person. And that’s a lot of work outside of uncompensated work…But bridging that gap between what a student produces at the end of a semester and what can actually be published is still a big gap that needs to be addressed. And aside from the editing work, faculty described the administrative burden of being the connective tissue joining two distinct worlds. Negotiating pitches, coordinating deadlines, dealing with revisions, shepherding copy flows — all of it with students who are juggling other courses, work-study jobs, and the life of college student — can be “difficult to sustain without administrative support, dedicated staff, or funding for editors,” they write. Just to save a few dozen proud Mizzou alums from emailing me: Yes, the “teaching hospital” model is not a new idea. The idea of giving journalism students real-world experience in public-facing outlets has been central to the Missouri Method for more than a century, and other major journalism schools have long done versions of this work. But Bhowmik notes that many of these new efforts are in smaller colleges with smaller journalism programs — places without the in-house infrastructure of a Columbia Missourian or KOMU, where media partnerships involve more ongoing negotiation. That can leave more of the pressure of sustainability on one or two overworked faculty members. As Bhowmik and Lorenz put it: …faculty labor is not incidental to the model; it is the hidden infrastructure that allows student work to move from classroom assignment to public-facing journalism. We argue that passion may help launch these programs, but passion alone is not a sustainable staffing model. If universities treat these initiatives as ordinary courses while expecting faculty to function simultaneously as instructors, editors, partnership managers, and newsroom supervisors, the model remains vulnerable to burnout and institutional instability. None of this, of course, is to say that these academic-publisher partnerships aren’t performing valuable roles in hundreds of communities. These programs have generated thousands of stories that otherwise might never have reached the public. But the sort of exhaustion Bhowmik and Lorenz describe reminds me a lot of what we saw in the first wave of local nonprofit news outlets in the late 2000s and early 2010s. Many of those were founded by idealistic journalists — often refugees from the local daily — who saw the opportunity to do something new and valuable for their communities. They put in long hours editing stories, selling ads, pitching foundations, and building audiences. And within a few years, a lot of them were burnt out, and their outlets suffered for it. In the decade-plus since, funders have gotten smarter about building institutional capacity — knowing that passion can only fuel so much. The world Bhowmik and Lorenz describe inside these journalism programs makes me think that department chairs and deans would be wise to consider a similar pivot to make these academic-publisher partnerships sustainable for the long term.

“What’s happening in the academic ecosystem is so phenomenal, thanks to these really passionate founders on the faculty,” Bhowmik told me. “But the extra work they’re putting it isn’t always getting acknowledged within the system. A professor might be editing 10 or 20 stories a week, by students without much experience, on top of a full teaching load. There’s a lot of overwork and some dissatisfaction, even though they know how important the work is.”

Here’s how one faculty member described their situation:

Think about it: Like, how many reporters does the average assigning editor at a mid-sized newspaper supervise? Half a dozen? So try, like, 20 of them, and each of them is writing for a different outlet maybe, which has its own set of deadlines and editor. And they’re all at varying skill levels, and broadly, they’re all pretty new. Their copy requires a lot of work, their reporting often requires a lot of handholding. Another talked about the responsibility of getting student work up to the level a news outlet expects. You can’t just give a meh student story a C- and be done with it if a local editor is expecting publishable copy. It takes a lot of work to get student work up to the quality of professionally published work. And a lot of students start out with really good ideas, do some good reporting by the time they’re done in the semester, it needs work, and that tends to fall on the faculty person. And that’s a lot of work outside of uncompensated work…But bridging that gap between what a student produces at the end of a semester and what can actually be published is still a big gap that needs to be addressed. And aside from the editing work, faculty described the administrative burden of being the connective tissue joining two distinct worlds. Negotiating pitches, coordinating deadlines, dealing with revisions, shepherding copy flows — all of it with students who are juggling other courses, work-study jobs, and the life of college student — can be “difficult to sustain without administrative support, dedicated staff, or funding for editors,” they write. Just to save a few dozen proud Mizzou alums from emailing me: Yes, the “teaching hospital” model is not a new idea. The idea of giving journalism students real-world experience in public-facing outlets has been central to the Missouri Method for more than a century, and other major journalism schools have long done versions of this work. But Bhowmik notes that many of these new efforts are in smaller colleges with smaller journalism programs — places without the in-house infrastructure of a Columbia Missourian or KOMU, where media partnerships involve more ongoing negotiation. That can leave more of the pressure of sustainability on one or two overworked faculty members. As Bhowmik and Lorenz put it: …faculty labor is not incidental to the model; it is the hidden infrastructure that allows student work to move from classroom assignment to public-facing journalism. We argue that passion may help launch these programs, but passion alone is not a sustainable staffing model. If universities treat these initiatives as ordinary courses while expecting faculty to function simultaneously as instructors, editors, partnership managers, and newsroom supervisors, the model remains vulnerable to burnout and institutional instability. None of this, of course, is to say that these academic-publisher partnerships aren’t performing valuable roles in hundreds of communities. These programs have generated thousands of stories that otherwise might never have reached the public. But the sort of exhaustion Bhowmik and Lorenz describe reminds me a lot of what we saw in the first wave of local nonprofit news outlets in the late 2000s and early 2010s. Many of those were founded by idealistic journalists — often refugees from the local daily — who saw the opportunity to do something new and valuable for their communities. They put in long hours editing stories, selling ads, pitching foundations, and building audiences. And within a few years, a lot of them were burnt out, and their outlets suffered for it.
